  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF20.9, F22\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, it’s nice to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, hello there, Doctor. I... I came down here today because, see, the men at the domino table have been giving me these little messages. Just meant for me. And sometimes the radio announcer does it too, warning me to be careful. \n\nMy wife, she says it’s just talk, but... well, she don't listen to the radio like I do. We got this old Zenith radio, you know, the big wooden kind? Bought it back in... oh, must've been '72 or '73. Still works real good. Catches the AM stations clear as a bell, especially on a cold morning. I used to tinker with radios a bit. Never had the proper schooling for it, you know, I had to leave school in the eighth grade to help my daddy out on the farm. But you learn by doing. \n\nAnyway, she was making her beef stew yesterday—she always puts too much pepper in it now, her taster's going—and I told her to listen to the man on the broadcast, but she just stirred the pot and told me it's nonsense. But I know they're warning me, Doctor. I know what I hear.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been receiving these messages from the domino players and the radio announcer?\n\nPatient: Let me think on that... it must be, oh, about nine months now. On and off, you know. It started right around the time the transmission went out on my grandson Jimmy's truck. He brought it over to the house, thinking I could fix it. I told him I ain't a mechanic, I just tinker with small engines. Lawnmowers, mostly. Those old Briggs and Stratton engines, they are just the easiest things to work on. You just clean the carburetor out with a little spray and they fire right up on the first pull. Used to do all the neighbors' mowers for a few dollars. \n\nAnyway, Jimmy ended up having to tow his truck to that shop down on Elm Street. Cost him a pretty penny, too. Young folks today, they just don't know how to save their money. Buy a cup of coffee for five dollars when you can make a whole pot at home for pennies. But yes sir, that is when I first started noticing the special messages and the voices. Right around nine months ago. Give or take.\n\nDoctor: Have you used any recreational drugs?\n\nPatient: Recreational drugs? Oh, Lord, no. Never touched anything like that in my life. I don't even smoke cigarettes anymore. Gave those up back in... well, it was the year they put the new roof on the Methodist church. Used to smoke Pall Malls, the unfiltered ones. My daddy smoked them, and I guess I just picked up the habit. \n\nBut no, no drugs. I take my blood pressure pill, and a little aspirin for my joints when the weather turns wet. You know, when that rain comes off the gulf, my left knee aches like nobody's business. I injured it falling off a tractor when I was nineteen. We were pulling stumps out of the lower pasture, and the chain snapped. Whipped right back and took my legs out from under me. I was lucky it didn't take my head off, honestly. The local doctor—old Dr. Henderson, he's passed on now, bless his soul—he wrapped it up real tight with some kind of liniment that smelled like wintergreen and horse sweat. Told me to stay off it for a month. Try telling a farmer to stay off his feet for a month! \n\nBut anyway, no sir, I don't mess with any of those illegal things. Just my regular medicine from the pharmacy.\n\nDoctor: How have these messages and voices affected your ability to manage your usual daily activities?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it has changed my routine some. Like... well, I've missed a few of my choir rehearsals lately. I sing bass down at the Methodist church. Been singing there since... oh, must be fifty years now. We used to have Mrs. Gable directing, and she played the piano so hard she'd bounce right up off the bench! Now they got this young fella who wants us singing all these new songs. I keep telling him, you just can't beat \"How Great Thou Art.\" Nothing sounds better than a full choir on that chorus. But anyway, I just haven't been going because it's hard to focus on the music with everything else going on. \n\nAnd my garden, well... I've let parts of it just go to weed this year. I usually grow a big patch of tomatoes. Those Big Boy tomatoes, you know? Best thing in the world is a fresh tomato sandwich with a little mayonnaise and black pepper on soft white bread. My daddy taught me to bury crushed eggshells under the roots to stop the bottoms from rotting out. Works like a charm. It's the calcium, I reckon. But I just haven't had the mind to get out there in the dirt and tend to it all like I should. \n\nAnd the domino games... well, I guess I made things a bit uncomfortable down at the tables. When I started hearing the men sending me those little warnings, I'd call them out on it, and... well, it got real tense. So I just don't join in the games like I used to. Earl still waves when I walk by, but I just keep on walking. He always was a cheat at dominoes anyway, holding onto that double-six till the very end... but no, I don't sit with them much anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any recent problems with your memory or thinking?\n\nPatient: Memory? No, no, I don't think so. My mind's still as sharp as a tack, far as I can tell. I remember things just fine. Like the other day, I was trying to remember the name of that fella who used to run the hardware store down on Main Street. Not the new one with the bright orange sign, but the old one, before the big storm took the roof off. You know the storm I'm talking about? Back in '78, I think it was. Blew the old oak tree right over onto Mrs. Higgins' porch. She was so upset about that tree. Said her grandfather planted it when he came back from the war. \n\nAnyway, the hardware store fella... his name was Arthur! Arthur Pendelton. Sold me my first set of socket wrenches. Heavy things, real steel, not like the cheap metal they use nowadays. You'd strip a bolt with these new ones before you even got it tight. Arthur always had a jar of peppermint candies on the counter for the kids. The ones with the red stripes. I used to get one every time my daddy went in for nails. We were building a new chicken coop out back because the old one got into by a fox. Sneaky things, foxes. You gotta bury the chicken wire at least a foot down or they'll just dig right under it. \n\nBut no, my memory is just fine. I haven't noticed any trouble with it at all.\n\nDoctor: How old are you?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm eighty-four years old now. Eighty-four. Sometimes I can hardly believe it myself. Seems like just yesterday I was turning sixty and thinking that was old! We had a big barbecue for my sixtieth. Roasted a whole pig right out there in the yard. My brother-in-law, Thomas—he was married to my sister Sarah, God rest her soul—he built this cinderblock pit just for the occasion. Took him three days to get the draft right on that thing. He was always tinkering with stuff like that. Used to work down at the railyard before they shut it down in the nineties. \n\nAnyway, that pig was something else. We had to baste it with a mop! Just a regular cotton mop dipped in a bucket of vinegar and pepper sauce. Best barbecue I ever had. People still talk about it sometimes, the ones that are left around here. But yeah, eighty-four. Born in the spring, right around the time the bluebonnets start coming up along the highway. You ever notice how they always grow thickest right there on the shoulder? I always thought it was the wind from the cars blowing the seeds around, but who knows.\n\nDoctor: Did these experiences begin after any significant change in your health?\n\nPatient: Well, let's see... a change in my health? No, my health's been about the same. But I can tell you exactly when all this started. It's been about a year and five months now. Started right after my good friend Charlie passed away. Oh, Charlie... we knew each other since we were knee-high to a grasshopper. We used to go fishing down at Miller's Pond every single Saturday, rain or shine. He had this old aluminum johnboat that leaked just a little bit right near the transom. Not enough to sink you, but you had to keep a coffee tin handy to bail it out every hour or so. \n\nHe always swore by those jitterbug lures. The green ones with the little yellow spots. Said the bass couldn't resist the sound they made popping across the top of the water. I always preferred a simple worm and bobber myself. There's something peaceful about just watching that red and white plastic bobbing out there, waiting for it to dip under. We'd sit out there for hours, drinking RC Cola and eating those little Debbie oatmeal cream pies. You know the ones? They used to be a lot bigger back in the day. Everything's getting smaller now, it seems. Even the candy bars at the checkout line. \n\nBut yeah, it was right after Charlie's funeral that all this started up. About a year and five months ago. We buried him out at the Restland Cemetery, right near that big weeping willow tree. Beautiful spot, really. The shade keeps the grass nice and green all summer long.\n\nDoctor: Has a doctor ever told you that you have a neurological or brain condition?\n\nPatient: A brain condition? No, no sir. Never had any doctor tell me anything like that. I mean, old Doc Evans—he was the one who delivered half the county, you know—he did have to patch up my head once. But that was just a cut. Must've been... oh, the summer of '82, I reckon. I was working on the old John Deere combine out in the field. The green ones, you know? Not the big fancy machines they have now with the air-conditioned cabs and the satellite radios. Just a metal seat that'd burn the hide right off you if you left it sitting in the sun too long. You had to throw a burlap sack over it just to sit down. \n\nAnyway, I slipped off the ladder and whacked my head right on the hitch. Bled like a stuck pig. Head wounds always do, they say. Doc Evans put about eight stitches right here above my ear. He used this thick black thread, looked just like heavy-test fishing line. He always smelled like cherry pipe tobacco and rubbing alcohol, Doc Evans did. He'd smoke a corn cob pipe right there in the examination room while he was sewing you up! Can you imagine that today? They'd probably shut the whole clinic down for that now. But no, he never said nothing about my actual brain being bad. Just told me to keep the stitches clean and stay out of the midday sun for a spell.\n\nDoctor: Have you recently become confused about where you are?\n\nPatient: Confused about where I am? No, I always know where I'm at. I've lived in this county my whole life, so there ain't a road or a dirt path I don't recognize. Even when they put in that new bypass over by the old Miller farm—you know the one, where they tore down that beautiful red barn? Such a shame, too. That barn was built back in the twenties. They used real wooden pegs instead of nails for the main beams. My daddy used to say a barn built with pegs would sway in a tornado but it wouldn't snap. \n\nI remember when we used to go out there to pick up hay. We'd stack those square bales so high on the flatbed you'd think the whole truck was gonna tip over going around a bend. You had to wear long sleeves, even in August, or that hay would scratch your arms up something awful. And the dust! You'd be sneezing for two days straight after hauling hay. We'd always stop at the filling station on the way back and get a cold bottle of Nehi Grape. Nothing tasted better than that cold grape soda after sweating in a hayfield all afternoon. But no, I don't get confused about where I am. I know exactly where I am.\n\nDoctor: Has your wife noticed any decline in your memory recently?\n\nPatient: My memory? No, she hasn't said anything about my memory slipping. Martha's usually pretty quick to point out if I forget something, like taking out the trash on Tuesday mornings. You know, the sanitation department changed their route a few years back. Used to be they'd come around on Thursdays, right about the time the mailman, Mr. Henderson, would drop off the post. \n\nMr. Henderson was a real character. He had this little white mail truck with the steering wheel on the wrong side. He'd always leave a milk bone in the mailbox for our old golden retriever, Buster. Best dog we ever had, Buster was. We found him wandering near the old train tracks on the edge of town, just a skinny little pup. Took him in, fed him some leftover meatloaf, and he never left our side after that. He loved riding in the back of my pickup truck, just letting his ears flap in the wind. We had a '78 Ford F-150 back then. Two-tone, brown and tan. Had a manual transmission with the shifter right there on the steering column. Three on the tree, they called it. You don't see those much anymore. Everybody wants an automatic now, so they can hold their coffee in one hand and their telephone in the other while they drive. But no, Martha hasn't mentioned anything about my memory.\n\nDoctor: Has a doctor found any medical or neurological condition that could explain these experiences?\n\nPatient: No, no doctor has ever said anything like that. They haven't found any medical reason for what I go through. I mean, they did all sorts of blood tests a while back, taking vials and vials of it. The nurse who drew my blood, she was a sweet girl. Had hair the color of spun gold, just like my niece Sarah. \n\nSarah used to come visit us every summer when she was little. We'd take her down to the creek to catch crawdads. You had to tie a piece of bacon to a string and just dangle it in the water, wait for them to pinch it. Then you'd scoop 'em up real quick with a net. We'd keep 'em in a galvanized bucket for a while, just to watch 'em, then toss 'em back. Sarah loved that. She's all grown up now, of course. Moved out to Seattle, works for one of those big computer companies. Sends us a Christmas card every year with a picture of her and her husband. They got a nice-looking house, big windows. But no, the doctors haven't found any physical sickness that would cause all this.\n\nDoctor: Do you hear voices speaking to you when you are alone and there is no radio or other person nearby?\n\nPatient: Voices? Well, yes, sometimes I do. Some afternoons, when the house is real quiet and Martha's out at her garden club, I hear my old friend Jimmy. He'll call my name from the front porch, or I'll hear him humming one of those old choir songs, clear as day, like he's standing right there next to me. \n\nJimmy had a beautiful baritone voice, you know. We used to sing in the church choir together back at First Methodist. The choir director, Mrs. Gable, she was a strict woman. Always wore these floral dresses with a lace collar and had her hair pinned up tight. If you missed a note, she'd tap her baton on the music stand so hard you'd think she was gonna snap it in two. But she knew how to get a good sound out of us. We did a Christmas cantata back in '65 that folks talked about for years. Jimmy had the solo on \"O Holy Night.\" He hit that high note at the end and I swear the stained glass windows rattled just a little bit. \n\nThose windows were something else, too. They were imported all the way from Italy when the church was built. Had the whole story of the Good Samaritan in the big one facing the street. I remember sitting in the pew as a boy, just watching the sunlight come through that red glass in the Samaritan's robe. It would cast this pink glow right across the wooden pews. They don't make glass like that anymore, all hand-blown and pieced together with lead. Takes a real craftsman to do that kind of work. But yes, I hear Jimmy humming sometimes when I'm all alone.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the messages and voices, have you noticed yourself becoming unusually disorganized—for example, having trouble keeping your thoughts connected or behaving in ways that seem confusing to others?\n\nPatient: Disorganized? Well, I don't know if I'd call it that, but the fellas down at the lodge have been giving me a hard time lately. We play dominoes every Thursday afternoon, have for years. Lately, I guess I've been... well, I'll start tapping the tiles, you know? Just tapping them in this specific pattern, over and over. And sometimes I'll hold my hand up, kind of like a crossing guard stopping traffic, right in the middle of a hand. The guys will be telling me, \"Come on, just play your tile,\" but I just... I have to do it that way. \n\nWe play on this old felt-covered table that used to belong to Arthur Pendelton. Arthur was a character. He owned the hardware store on Main Street, the one that burned down back in '92. Terribly sad thing, that fire. Started in the back room where they kept the paint thinner. The fire department was there for hours, spraying water all over the place. The chief back then was a big burly fella named O'Malley. He had this magnificent mustache, curled right up at the ends. Used wax on it every morning, he told me once. He was always chewing on an unlit cigar, too. Said he promised his wife he'd quit smoking, so he just chewed on 'em instead. I don't know if that's any better for you, honestly. But yeah, the dominoes... the fellas get a little confused when I start tapping and holding my hand up like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you hear voices even when you are not thinking about the messages from the domino players or the radio?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose I do... yes. The voices don't really care what I'm thinking about. I could be out on the porch, just watching the cars go by, not thinking about the dominoes or the radio at all, and Jimmy will still start humming. \n\nI was sitting out there just yesterday, actually. Watching Mr. Henderson's boy mow the lawn across the street. He uses one of those old push mowers, the kind with the spinning blades that go snick-snick-snick. It's good exercise for a boy his age. I had one just like it when I was a teenager. Used to cut grass all over the neighborhood for fifty cents a yard. Saved up enough to buy myself a genuine leather baseball mitt. The smell of that leather... there's nothing quite like it. I used to rub neat's-foot oil into it every night to keep it soft. Kept it under my mattress so it would form a perfect pocket. Played third base for the county league. We had a game against the boys from Oakhaven once, and I caught a line drive right down the third-base line that would've taken my head off if I hadn't put my glove up in time. The crowd went wild. My father was in the stands, wearing his good Sunday hat... but, what was I saying? Oh, right. No, the voices just come when they please, regardless of what's on my mind.\n\nDoctor: Have people told you recently that your speech is difficult to follow or that your thoughts seem to jump from one subject to another?\n\nPatient: Well, my daughter Martha mentioned something about that just the other morning over breakfast. She said she couldn't keep up with what I was saying. But it's just... my mind runs from the choir to baseball to repairs to my friend Jimmy and back again so fast that I cannot hold one thought long enough to explain it right. \n\nSpeaking of repairs, I was looking at the hinges on our screen door yesterday and they are getting awful rusty. You have to keep those oiled or they'll squeak every time the wind blows. My father used to keep a little can of 3-in-One oil on the top shelf in the mudroom just for things like that. That mudroom always smelled like wet wool and machine oil. We had this big old sheepdog named Barnaby who used to sleep out there on an old braided rug. Best dog you could ever ask for, though he had a terrible habit of chasing the mailman. \n\nThe mail carrier back then was a fella named Mr. Higgins. He wore this smart little gray uniform with a cap, and he always kept a pocketful of dog biscuits to keep Barnaby at bay. Higgins had a bit of a limp from the war, you know. Served over in the Pacific. He brought back a real Japanese compass and showed it to me once when I was a boy... but, yes, I suppose my thoughts do jump around a bit too fast for folks to follow sometimes.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

He always was a cheat at dominoes anyway, holding onto that double-six till the very end... but no, I don't sit with them much anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any recent problems with your memory or thinking?\n\nPatient: Memory? No, no, I don't think so. My mind's still as sharp as a tack, far as I can tell. I remember things just fine. Like the other day, I was trying to remember the name of that fella who used to run the hardware store down on Main Street. Not the new one with the bright orange sign, but the old one, before the big storm took the roof off. You know the storm I'm talking about? Back in '78, I think it was. Blew the old oak tree right over onto Mrs. Higgins' porch. She was so upset about that tree. Said her grandfather planted it when he came back from the war. \n\nAnyway, the hardware store fella... his name was Arthur! Arthur Pendelton. Sold me my first set of socket wrenches. Heavy things, real steel, not like the cheap metal they use nowadays. You'd strip a bolt with these new ones before you even got it tight. Arthur always had a jar of peppermint candies on the counter for the kids. The ones with the red stripes. I used to get one every time my daddy went in for nails. We were building a new chicken coop out back because the old one got into by a fox. Sneaky things, foxes. You gotta bury the chicken wire at least a foot down or they'll just dig right under it. \n\nBut no, my memory is just fine. I haven't noticed any trouble with it at all.\n\nDoctor: How old are you?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm eighty-four years old now. Eighty-four. Sometimes I can hardly believe it myself. Seems like just yesterday I was turning sixty and thinking that was old! We had a big barbecue for my sixtieth. Roasted a whole pig right out there in the yard. My brother-in-law, Thomas—he was married to my sister Sarah, God rest her soul—he built this cinderblock pit just for the occasion. Took him three days to get the draft right on that thing. He was always tinkering with stuff like that. Used to work down at the railyard before they shut it down in the nineties. \n\nAnyway, that pig was something else. We had to baste it with a mop! Just a regular cotton mop dipped in a bucket of vinegar and pepper sauce. Best barbecue I ever had. People still talk about it sometimes, the ones that are left around here. But yeah, eighty-four. Born in the spring, right around the time the bluebonnets start coming up along the highway. You ever notice how they always grow thickest right there on the shoulder? I always thought it was the wind from the cars blowing the seeds around, but who knows.\n\nDoctor: Did these experiences begin after any significant change in your health?\n\nPatient: Well, let's see... a change in my health? No, my health's been about the same. But I can tell you exactly when all this started. It's been about a year and five months now. Started right after my good friend Charlie passed away. Oh, Charlie... we knew each other since we were knee-high to a grasshopper. We used to go fishing down at Miller's Pond every single Saturday, rain or shine. He had this old aluminum johnboat that leaked just a little bit right near the transom. Not enough to sink you, but you had to keep a coffee tin handy to bail it out every hour or so. \n\nHe always swore by those jitterbug lures. The green ones with the little yellow spots. Said the bass couldn't resist the sound they made popping across the top of the water. I always preferred a simple worm and bobber myself. There's something peaceful about just watching that red and white plastic bobbing out there, waiting for it to dip under. We'd sit out there for hours, drinking RC Cola and eating those little Debbie oatmeal cream pies. You know the ones? They used to be a lot bigger back in the day. Everything's getting smaller now, it seems. Even the candy bars at the checkout line. \n\nBut yeah, it was right after Charlie's funeral that all this started up. About a year and five months ago. We buried him out at the Restland Cemetery, right near that big weeping willow tree. Beautiful spot, really. The shade keeps the grass nice and green all summer long.\n\nDoctor: Has a doctor ever told you that you have a neurological or brain condition?\n\nPatient: A brain condition? No, no sir. Never had any doctor tell me anything like that. I mean, old Doc Evans—he was the one who delivered half the county, you know—he did have to patch up my head once. But that was just a cut. Must've been... oh, the summer of '82, I reckon. I was working on the old John Deere combine out in the field. The green ones, you know? Not the big fancy machines they have now with the air-conditioned cabs and the satellite radios. Just a metal seat that'd burn the hide right off you if you left it sitting in the sun too long. You had to throw a burlap sack over it just to sit down. \n\nAnyway, I slipped off the ladder and whacked my head right on the hitch. Bled like a stuck pig. Head wounds always do, they say. Doc Evans put about eight stitches right here above my ear. He used this thick black thread, looked just like heavy-test fishing line. He always smelled like cherry pipe tobacco and rubbing alcohol, Doc Evans did. He'd smoke a corn cob pipe right there in the examination room while he was sewing you up! Can you imagine that today? They'd probably shut the whole clinic down for that now. But no, he never said nothing about my actual brain being bad. Just told me to keep the stitches clean and stay out of the midday sun for a spell.\n\nDoctor: Have you recently become confused about where you are?\n\nPatient: Confused about where I am? No, I always know where I'm at. I've lived in this county my whole life, so there ain't a road or a dirt path I don't recognize. Even when they put in that new bypass over by the old Miller farm—you know the one, where they tore down that beautiful red barn? Such a shame, too. That barn was built back in the twenties. They used real wooden pegs instead of nails for the main beams. My daddy used to say a barn built with pegs would sway in a tornado but it wouldn't snap. \n\nI remember when we used to go out there to pick up hay. We'd stack those square bales so high on the flatbed you'd think the whole truck was gonna tip over going around a bend. You had to wear long sleeves, even in August, or that hay would scratch your arms up something awful. And the dust! You'd be sneezing for two days straight after hauling hay. We'd always stop at the filling station on the way back and get a cold bottle of Nehi Grape. Nothing tasted better than that cold grape soda after sweating in a hayfield all afternoon. But no, I don't get confused about where I am. I know exactly where I am.\n\nDoctor: Has your wife noticed any decline in your memory recently?\n\nPatient: My memory? No, she hasn't said anything about my memory slipping. Martha's usually pretty quick to point out if I forget something, like taking out the trash on Tuesday mornings. You know, the sanitation department changed their route a few years back. Used to be they'd come around on Thursdays, right about the time the mailman, Mr. Henderson, would drop off the post. \n\nMr. Henderson was a real character. He had this little white mail truck with the steering wheel on the wrong side. He'd always leave a milk bone in the mailbox for our old golden retriever, Buster. Best dog we ever had, Buster was. We found him wandering near the old train tracks on the edge of town, just a skinny little pup. Took him in, fed him some leftover meatloaf, and he never left our side after that. He loved riding in the back of my pickup truck, just letting his ears flap in the wind. We had a '78 Ford F-150 back then. Two-tone, brown and tan. Had a manual transmission with the shifter right there on the steering column. Three on the tree, they called it. You don't see those much anymore. Everybody wants an automatic now, so they can hold their coffee in one hand and their telephone in the other while they drive. But no, Martha hasn't mentioned anything about my memory.\n\nDoctor: Has a doctor found any medical or neurological condition that could explain these experiences?\n\nPatient: No, no doctor has ever said anything like that. They haven't found any medical reason for what I go through. I mean, they did all sorts of blood tests a while back, taking vials and vials of it. The nurse who drew my blood, she was a sweet girl. Had hair the color of spun gold, just like my niece Sarah. \n\nSarah used to come visit us every summer when she was little. We'd take her down to the creek to catch crawdads. You had to tie a piece of bacon to a string and just dangle it in the water, wait for them to pinch it. Then you'd scoop 'em up real quick with a net. We'd keep 'em in a galvanized bucket for a while, just to watch 'em, then toss 'em back. Sarah loved that. She's all grown up now, of course. Moved out to Seattle, works for one of those big computer companies. Sends us a Christmas card every year with a picture of her and her husband. They got a nice-looking house, big windows. But no, the doctors haven't found any physical sickness that would cause all this.\n\nDoctor: Do you hear voices speaking to you when you are alone and there is no radio or other person nearby?\n\nPatient: Voices? Well, yes, sometimes I do. Some afternoons, when the house is real quiet and Martha's out at her garden club, I hear my old friend Jimmy. He'll call my name from the front porch, or I'll hear him humming one of those old choir songs, clear as day, like he's standing right there next to me. \n\nJimmy had a beautiful baritone voice, you know. We used to sing in the church choir together back at First Methodist. The choir director, Mrs. Gable, she was a strict woman. Always wore these floral dresses with a lace collar and had her hair pinned up tight. If you missed a note, she'd tap her baton on the music stand so hard you'd think she was gonna snap it in two. But she knew how to get a good sound out of us. We did a Christmas cantata back in '65 that folks talked about for years. Jimmy had the solo on \"O Holy Night.\" He hit that high note at the end and I swear the stained glass windows rattled just a little bit. \n\nThose windows were something else, too. They were imported all the way from Italy when the church was built. Had the whole story of the Good Samaritan in the big one facing the street. I remember sitting in the pew as a boy, just watching the sunlight come through that red glass in the Samaritan's robe. It would cast this pink glow right across the wooden pews. They don't make glass like that anymore, all hand-blown and pieced together with lead. Takes a real craftsman to do that kind of work. But yes, I hear Jimmy humming sometimes when I'm all alone.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the messages and voices, have you noticed yourself becoming unusually disorganized—for example, having trouble keeping your thoughts connected or behaving in ways that seem confusing to others?\n\nPatient: Disorganized? Well, I don't know if I'd call it that, but the fellas down at the lodge have been giving me a hard time lately. We play dominoes every Thursday afternoon, have for years. Lately, I guess I've been... well, I'll start tapping the tiles, you know? Just tapping them in this specific pattern, over and over. And sometimes I'll hold my hand up, kind of like a crossing guard stopping traffic, right in the middle of a hand. The guys will be telling me, \"Come on, just play your tile,\" but I just... I have to do it that way. \n\nWe play on this old felt-covered table that used to belong to Arthur Pendelton. Arthur was a character. He owned the hardware store on Main Street, the one that burned down back in '92. Terribly sad thing, that fire. Started in the back room where they kept the paint thinner. The fire department was there for hours, spraying water all over the place. The chief back then was a big burly fella named O'Malley. He had this magnificent mustache, curled right up at the ends. Used wax on it every morning, he told me once. He was always chewing on an unlit cigar, too. Said he promised his wife he'd quit smoking, so he just chewed on 'em instead. I don't know if that's any better for you, honestly. But yeah, the dominoes... the fellas get a little confused when I start tapping and holding my hand up like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you hear voices even when you are not thinking about the messages from the domino players or the radio?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose I do... yes. The voices don't really care what I'm thinking about. I could be out on the porch, just watching the cars go by, not thinking about the dominoes or the radio at all, and Jimmy will still start humming. \n\nI was sitting out there just yesterday, actually. Watching Mr. Henderson's boy mow the lawn across the street. He uses one of those old push mowers, the kind with the spinning blades that go snick-snick-snick. It's good exercise for a boy his age. I had one just like it when I was a teenager. Used to cut grass all over the neighborhood for fifty cents a yard. Saved up enough to buy myself a genuine leather baseball mitt. The smell of that leather... there's nothing quite like it. I used to rub neat's-foot oil into it every night to keep it soft. Kept it under my mattress so it would form a perfect pocket. Played third base for the county league. We had a game against the boys from Oakhaven once, and I caught a line drive right down the third-base line that would've taken my head off if I hadn't put my glove up in time. The crowd went wild. My father was in the stands, wearing his good Sunday hat... but, what was I saying? Oh, right. No, the voices just come when they please, regardless of what's on my mind.\n\nDoctor: Have people told you recently that your speech is difficult to follow or that your thoughts seem to jump from one subject to another?\n\nPatient: Well, my daughter Martha mentioned something about that just the other morning over breakfast. She said she couldn't keep up with what I was saying. But it's just... my mind runs from the choir to baseball to repairs to my friend Jimmy and back again so fast that I cannot hold one thought long enough to explain it right. \n\nSpeaking of repairs, I was looking at the hinges on our screen door yesterday and they are getting awful rusty. You have to keep those oiled or they'll squeak every time the wind blows. My father used to keep a little can of 3-in-One oil on the top shelf in the mudroom just for things like that. That mudroom always smelled like wet wool and machine oil. We had this big old sheepdog named Barnaby who used to sleep out there on an old braided rug. Best dog you could ever ask for, though he had a terrible habit of chasing the mailman. \n\nThe mail carrier back then was a fella named Mr. Higgins. He wore this smart little gray uniform with a cap, and he always kept a pocketful of dog biscuits to keep Barnaby at bay. Higgins had a bit of a limp from the war, you know. Served over in the Pacific. He brought back a real Japanese compass and showed it to me once when I was a boy... but, yes, I suppose my thoughts do jump around a bit too fast for folks to follow sometimes.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

No, the voices just come when they please, regardless of what's on my mind.\n\nDoctor: Have people told you recently that your speech is difficult to follow or that your thoughts seem to jump from one subject to another?\n\nPatient: Well, my daughter Martha mentioned something about that just the other morning over breakfast. She said she couldn't keep up with what I was saying. But it's just... my mind runs from the choir to baseball to repairs to my friend Jimmy and back again so fast that I cannot hold one thought long enough to explain it right. \n\nSpeaking of repairs, I was looking at the hinges on our screen door yesterday and they are getting awful rusty. You have to keep those oiled or they'll squeak every time the wind blows. My father used to keep a little can of 3-in-One oil on the top shelf in the mudroom just for things like that. That mudroom always smelled like wet wool and machine oil. We had this big old sheepdog named Barnaby who used to sleep out there on an old braided rug. Best dog you could ever ask for, though he had a terrible habit of chasing the mailman. \n\nThe mail carrier back then was a fella named Mr. Higgins. He wore this smart little gray uniform with a cap, and he always kept a pocketful of dog biscuits to keep Barnaby at bay. Higgins had a bit of a limp from the war, you know. Served over in the Pacific. He brought back a real Japanese compass and showed it to me once when I was a boy... but, yes, I suppose my thoughts do jump around a bit too fast for folks to follow sometimes.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you had trouble managing tasks you used to handle independently, such as paying bills, taking medications, or getting dressed?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 84 / not_in_workforce / less_than_9th\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"I have missed choir rehearsals, let parts of my garden go, and made the domino games uncomfortable enough that I do not join like I used to.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, managing things... I suppose some things have slipped a bit. I haven't been going to choir rehearsals lately, which is a shame. And I've let parts of my garden go to weed. Plus, like I said, I've made the domino games uncomfortable enough that I just don't join in like I used to. 

I used to grow the most beautiful prize-winning tomatoes in that garden. Big, juicy Beefsteaks. My wife, Lord rest her soul, used to make the most wonderful tomato preserves. People think tomato preserves sound strange, but you put a little ginger and lemon in there, and it's just delightful on a piece of toast. We had this old toaster, a heavy chrome thing that you had to flip the sides down to turn the bread. You had to watch it like a hawk or it would burn your toast to a crisp. My brother Henry burned a piece so bad once, it set off the smoke alarm and the fire department came. It wasn't O'Malley that time, though, it was a younger fellow... but yes, I guess I haven't been keeping up with things as well as I should.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF20.9, F22, F03.90\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, it’s nice to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, hello there, Doctor. I... I came down here today because, see, the men at the domino table have been giving me these little messages. Just meant for me. And sometimes the radio announcer does it too, warning me to be careful. \n\nMy wife, she says it’s just talk, but... well, she don't listen to the radio like I do. We got this old Zenith radio, you know, the big wooden kind? Bought it back in... oh, must've been '72 or '73. Still works real good. Catches the AM stations clear as a bell, especially on a cold morning. I used to tinker with radios a bit. Never had the proper schooling for it, you know, I had to leave school in the eighth grade to help my daddy out on the farm. But you learn by doing. \n\nAnyway, she was making her beef stew yesterday—she always puts too much pepper in it now, her taster's going—and I told her to listen to the man on the broadcast, but she just stirred the pot and told me it's nonsense. But I know they're warning me, Doctor. I know what I hear.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been receiving these messages from the domino players and the radio announcer?\n\nPatient: Let me think on that... it must be, oh, about nine months now. On and off, you know. It started right around the time the transmission went out on my grandson Jimmy's truck. He brought it over to the house, thinking I could fix it. I told him I ain't a mechanic, I just tinker with small engines. Lawnmowers, mostly. Those old Briggs and Stratton engines, they are just the easiest things to work on. You just clean the carburetor out with a little spray and they fire right up on the first pull. Used to do all the neighbors' mowers for a few dollars. \n\nAnyway, Jimmy ended up having to tow his truck to that shop down on Elm Street. Cost him a pretty penny, too. Young folks today, they just don't know how to save their money. Buy a cup of coffee for five dollars when you can make a whole pot at home for pennies. But yes sir, that is when I first started noticing the special messages and the voices. Right around nine months ago. Give or take.\n\nDoctor: Have you used any recreational drugs?\n\nPatient: Recreational drugs? Oh, Lord, no. Never touched anything like that in my life. I don't even smoke cigarettes anymore. Gave those up back in... well, it was the year they put the new roof on the Methodist church. Used to smoke Pall Malls, the unfiltered ones. My daddy smoked them, and I guess I just picked up the habit. \n\nBut no, no drugs. I take my blood pressure pill, and a little aspirin for my joints when the weather turns wet. You know, when that rain comes off the gulf, my left knee aches like nobody's business. I injured it falling off a tractor when I was nineteen. We were pulling stumps out of the lower pasture, and the chain snapped. Whipped right back and took my legs out from under me. I was lucky it didn't take my head off, honestly. The local doctor—old Dr. Henderson, he's passed on now, bless his soul—he wrapped it up real tight with some kind of liniment that smelled like wintergreen and horse sweat. Told me to stay off it for a month. Try telling a farmer to stay off his feet for a month! \n\nBut anyway, no sir, I don't mess with any of those illegal things. Just my regular medicine from the pharmacy.\n\nDoctor: How have these messages and voices affected your ability to manage your usual daily activities?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it has changed my routine some. Like... well, I've missed a few of my choir rehearsals lately. I sing bass down at the Methodist church. Been singing there since... oh, must be fifty years now. We used to have Mrs. Gable directing, and she played the piano so hard she'd bounce right up off the bench! Now they got this young fella who wants us singing all these new songs. I keep telling him, you just can't beat \"How Great Thou Art.\" Nothing sounds better than a full choir on that chorus. But anyway, I just haven't been going because it's hard to focus on the music with everything else going on. \n\nAnd my garden, well... I've let parts of it just go to weed this year. I usually grow a big patch of tomatoes. Those Big Boy tomatoes, you know? Best thing in the world is a fresh tomato sandwich with a little mayonnaise and black pepper on soft white bread. My daddy taught me to bury crushed eggshells under the roots to stop the bottoms from rotting out. Works like a charm. It's the calcium, I reckon. But I just haven't had the mind to get out there in the dirt and tend to it all like I should. \n\nAnd the domino games... well, I guess I made things a bit uncomfortable down at the tables. When I started hearing the men sending me those little warnings, I'd call them out on it, and... well, it got real tense. So I just don't join in the games like I used to. Earl still waves when I walk by, but I just keep on walking. He always was a cheat at dominoes anyway, holding onto that double-six till the very end... but no, I don't sit with them much anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any recent problems with your memory or thinking?\n\nPatient: Memory? No, no, I don't think so. My mind's still as sharp as a tack, far as I can tell. I remember things just fine. Like the other day, I was trying to remember the name of that fella who used to run the hardware store down on Main Street. Not the new one with the bright orange sign, but the old one, before the big storm took the roof off. You know the storm I'm talking about? Back in '78, I think it was. Blew the old oak tree right over onto Mrs. Higgins' porch. She was so upset about that tree. Said her grandfather planted it when he came back from the war. \n\nAnyway, the hardware store fella... his name was Arthur! Arthur Pendelton. Sold me my first set of socket wrenches. Heavy things, real steel, not like the cheap metal they use nowadays. You'd strip a bolt with these new ones before you even got it tight. Arthur always had a jar of peppermint candies on the counter for the kids. The ones with the red stripes. I used to get one every time my daddy went in for nails. We were building a new chicken coop out back because the old one got into by a fox. Sneaky things, foxes. You gotta bury the chicken wire at least a foot down or they'll just dig right under it. \n\nBut no, my memory is just fine. I haven't noticed any trouble with it at all.\n\nDoctor: How old are you?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm eighty-four years old now. Eighty-four. Sometimes I can hardly believe it myself. Seems like just yesterday I was turning sixty and thinking that was old! We had a big barbecue for my sixtieth. Roasted a whole pig right out there in the yard. My brother-in-law, Thomas—he was married to my sister Sarah, God rest her soul—he built this cinderblock pit just for the occasion. Took him three days to get the draft right on that thing. He was always tinkering with stuff like that. Used to work down at the railyard before they shut it down in the nineties. \n\nAnyway, that pig was something else. We had to baste it with a mop! Just a regular cotton mop dipped in a bucket of vinegar and pepper sauce. Best barbecue I ever had. People still talk about it sometimes, the ones that are left around here. But yeah, eighty-four. Born in the spring, right around the time the bluebonnets start coming up along the highway. You ever notice how they always grow thickest right there on the shoulder? I always thought it was the wind from the cars blowing the seeds around, but who knows.\n\nDoctor: Did these experiences begin after any significant change in your health?\n\nPatient: Well, let's see... a change in my health? No, my health's been about the same. But I can tell you exactly when all this started. It's been about a year and five months now. Started right after my good friend Charlie passed away. Oh, Charlie... we knew each other since we were knee-high to a grasshopper. We used to go fishing down at Miller's Pond every single Saturday, rain or shine. He had this old aluminum johnboat that leaked just a little bit right near the transom. Not enough to sink you, but you had to keep a coffee tin handy to bail it out every hour or so. \n\nHe always swore by those jitterbug lures. The green ones with the little yellow spots. Said the bass couldn't resist the sound they made popping across the top of the water. I always preferred a simple worm and bobber myself. There's something peaceful about just watching that red and white plastic bobbing out there, waiting for it to dip under. We'd sit out there for hours, drinking RC Cola and eating those little Debbie oatmeal cream pies. You know the ones? They used to be a lot bigger back in the day. Everything's getting smaller now, it seems. Even the candy bars at the checkout line. \n\nBut yeah, it was right after Charlie's funeral that all this started up. About a year and five months ago. We buried him out at the Restland Cemetery, right near that big weeping willow tree. Beautiful spot, really. The shade keeps the grass nice and green all summer long.\n\nDoctor: Has a doctor ever told you that you have a neurological or brain condition?\n\nPatient: A brain condition? No, no sir. Never had any doctor tell me anything like that. I mean, old Doc Evans—he was the one who delivered half the county, you know—he did have to patch up my head once. But that was just a cut. Must've been... oh, the summer of '82, I reckon. I was working on the old John Deere combine out in the field. The green ones, you know? Not the big fancy machines they have now with the air-conditioned cabs and the satellite radios. Just a metal seat that'd burn the hide right off you if you left it sitting in the sun too long. You had to throw a burlap sack over it just to sit down. \n\nAnyway, I slipped off the ladder and whacked my head right on the hitch. Bled like a stuck pig. Head wounds always do, they say. Doc Evans put about eight stitches right here above my ear. He used this thick black thread, looked just like heavy-test fishing line. He always smelled like cherry pipe tobacco and rubbing alcohol, Doc Evans did. He'd smoke a corn cob pipe right there in the examination room while he was sewing you up! Can you imagine that today? They'd probably shut the whole clinic down for that now. But no, he never said nothing about my actual brain being bad. Just told me to keep the stitches clean and stay out of the midday sun for a spell.\n\nDoctor: Have you recently become confused about where you are?\n\nPatient: Confused about where I am? No, I always know where I'm at. I've lived in this county my whole life, so there ain't a road or a dirt path I don't recognize. Even when they put in that new bypass over by the old Miller farm—you know the one, where they tore down that beautiful red barn? Such a shame, too. That barn was built back in the twenties. They used real wooden pegs instead of nails for the main beams. My daddy used to say a barn built with pegs would sway in a tornado but it wouldn't snap. \n\nI remember when we used to go out there to pick up hay. We'd stack those square bales so high on the flatbed you'd think the whole truck was gonna tip over going around a bend. You had to wear long sleeves, even in August, or that hay would scratch your arms up something awful. And the dust! You'd be sneezing for two days straight after hauling hay. We'd always stop at the filling station on the way back and get a cold bottle of Nehi Grape. Nothing tasted better than that cold grape soda after sweating in a hayfield all afternoon. But no, I don't get confused about where I am. I know exactly where I am.\n\nDoctor: Has your wife noticed any decline in your memory recently?\n\nPatient: My memory? No, she hasn't said anything about my memory slipping. Martha's usually pretty quick to point out if I forget something, like taking out the trash on Tuesday mornings. You know, the sanitation department changed their route a few years back. Used to be they'd come around on Thursdays, right about the time the mailman, Mr. Henderson, would drop off the post. \n\nMr. Henderson was a real character. He had this little white mail truck with the steering wheel on the wrong side. He'd always leave a milk bone in the mailbox for our old golden retriever, Buster. Best dog we ever had, Buster was. We found him wandering near the old train tracks on the edge of town, just a skinny little pup. Took him in, fed him some leftover meatloaf, and he never left our side after that. He loved riding in the back of my pickup truck, just letting his ears flap in the wind. We had a '78 Ford F-150 back then. Two-tone, brown and tan. Had a manual transmission with the shifter right there on the steering column. Three on the tree, they called it. You don't see those much anymore. Everybody wants an automatic now, so they can hold their coffee in one hand and their telephone in the other while they drive. But no, Martha hasn't mentioned anything about my memory.\n\nDoctor: Has a doctor found any medical or neurological condition that could explain these experiences?\n\nPatient: No, no doctor has ever said anything like that. They haven't found any medical reason for what I go through. I mean, they did all sorts of blood tests a while back, taking vials and vials of it. The nurse who drew my blood, she was a sweet girl. Had hair the color of spun gold, just like my niece Sarah. \n\nSarah used to come visit us every summer when she was little. We'd take her down to the creek to catch crawdads. You had to tie a piece of bacon to a string and just dangle it in the water, wait for them to pinch it. Then you'd scoop 'em up real quick with a net. We'd keep 'em in a galvanized bucket for a while, just to watch 'em, then toss 'em back. Sarah loved that. She's all grown up now, of course. Moved out to Seattle, works for one of those big computer companies. Sends us a Christmas card every year with a picture of her and her husband. They got a nice-looking house, big windows. But no, the doctors haven't found any physical sickness that would cause all this.\n\nDoctor: Do you hear voices speaking to you when you are alone and there is no radio or other person nearby?\n\nPatient: Voices? Well, yes, sometimes I do. Some afternoons, when the house is real quiet and Martha's out at her garden club, I hear my old friend Jimmy. He'll call my name from the front porch, or I'll hear him humming one of those old choir songs, clear as day, like he's standing right there next to me. \n\nJimmy had a beautiful baritone voice, you know. We used to sing in the church choir together back at First Methodist. The choir director, Mrs. Gable, she was a strict woman. Always wore these floral dresses with a lace collar and had her hair pinned up tight. If you missed a note, she'd tap her baton on the music stand so hard you'd think she was gonna snap it in two. But she knew how to get a good sound out of us. We did a Christmas cantata back in '65 that folks talked about for years. Jimmy had the solo on \"O Holy Night.\" He hit that high note at the end and I swear the stained glass windows rattled just a little bit. \n\nThose windows were something else, too. They were imported all the way from Italy when the church was built. Had the whole story of the Good Samaritan in the big one facing the street. I remember sitting in the pew as a boy, just watching the sunlight come through that red glass in the Samaritan's robe. It would cast this pink glow right across the wooden pews. They don't make glass like that anymore, all hand-blown and pieced together with lead. Takes a real craftsman to do that kind of work. But yes, I hear Jimmy humming sometimes when I'm all alone.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the messages and voices, have you noticed yourself becoming unusually disorganized—for example, having trouble keeping your thoughts connected or behaving in ways that seem confusing to others?\n\nPatient: Disorganized? Well, I don't know if I'd call it that, but the fellas down at the lodge have been giving me a hard time lately. We play dominoes every Thursday afternoon, have for years. Lately, I guess I've been... well, I'll start tapping the tiles, you know? Just tapping them in this specific pattern, over and over. And sometimes I'll hold my hand up, kind of like a crossing guard stopping traffic, right in the middle of a hand. The guys will be telling me, \"Come on, just play your tile,\" but I just... I have to do it that way. \n\nWe play on this old felt-covered table that used to belong to Arthur Pendelton. Arthur was a character. He owned the hardware store on Main Street, the one that burned down back in '92. Terribly sad thing, that fire. Started in the back room where they kept the paint thinner. The fire department was there for hours, spraying water all over the place. The chief back then was a big burly fella named O'Malley. He had this magnificent mustache, curled right up at the ends. Used wax on it every morning, he told me once. He was always chewing on an unlit cigar, too. Said he promised his wife he'd quit smoking, so he just chewed on 'em instead. I don't know if that's any better for you, honestly. But yeah, the dominoes... the fellas get a little confused when I start tapping and holding my hand up like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you hear voices even when you are not thinking about the messages from the domino players or the radio?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose I do... yes. The voices don't really care what I'm thinking about. I could be out on the porch, just watching the cars go by, not thinking about the dominoes or the radio at all, and Jimmy will still start humming. \n\nI was sitting out there just yesterday, actually. Watching Mr. Henderson's boy mow the lawn across the street. He uses one of those old push mowers, the kind with the spinning blades that go snick-snick-snick. It's good exercise for a boy his age. I had one just like it when I was a teenager. Used to cut grass all over the neighborhood for fifty cents a yard. Saved up enough to buy myself a genuine leather baseball mitt. The smell of that leather... there's nothing quite like it. I used to rub neat's-foot oil into it every night to keep it soft. Kept it under my mattress so it would form a perfect pocket. Played third base for the county league. We had a game against the boys from Oakhaven once, and I caught a line drive right down the third-base line that would've taken my head off if I hadn't put my glove up in time. The crowd went wild. My father was in the stands, wearing his good Sunday hat... but, what was I saying? Oh, right. No, the voices just come when they please, regardless of what's on my mind.\n\nDoctor: Have people told you recently that your speech is difficult to follow or that your thoughts seem to jump from one subject to another?\n\nPatient: Well, my daughter Martha mentioned something about that just the other morning over breakfast. She said she couldn't keep up with what I was saying. But it's just... my mind runs from the choir to baseball to repairs to my friend Jimmy and back again so fast that I cannot hold one thought long enough to explain it right. \n\nSpeaking of repairs, I was looking at the hinges on our screen door yesterday and they are getting awful rusty. You have to keep those oiled or they'll squeak every time the wind blows. My father used to keep a little can of 3-in-One oil on the top shelf in the mudroom just for things like that. That mudroom always smelled like wet wool and machine oil. We had this big old sheepdog named Barnaby who used to sleep out there on an old braided rug. Best dog you could ever ask for, though he had a terrible habit of chasing the mailman. \n\nThe mail carrier back then was a fella named Mr. Higgins. He wore this smart little gray uniform with a cap, and he always kept a pocketful of dog biscuits to keep Barnaby at bay. Higgins had a bit of a limp from the war, you know. Served over in the Pacific. He brought back a real Japanese compass and showed it to me once when I was a boy... but, yes, I suppose my thoughts do jump around a bit too fast for folks to follow sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble managing tasks you used to handle independently, such as paying bills, taking medications, or getting dressed?\n\nPatient: Well, managing things... I suppose some things have slipped a bit. I haven't been going to choir rehearsals lately, which is a shame. And I've let parts of my garden go to weed. Plus, like I said, I've made the domino games uncomfortable enough that I just don't join in like I used to. \n\nI used to grow the most beautiful prize-winning tomatoes in that garden. Big, juicy Beefsteaks. My wife, Lord rest her soul, used to make the most wonderful tomato preserves. People think tomato preserves sound strange, but you put a little ginger and lemon in there, and it's just delightful on a piece of toast. We had this old toaster, a heavy chrome thing that you had to flip the sides down to turn the bread. You had to watch it like a hawk or it would burn your toast to a crisp. My brother Henry burned a piece so bad once, it set off the smoke alarm and the fire department came. It wasn't O'Malley that time, though, it was a younger fellow... but yes, I guess I haven't been keeping up with things as well as I should.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
